# Rigveda Samhita — Volume 30 (Kannada → English translation)

## Preface (Pīṭhike)

### Printed p. i

In this part the last four chapters of the Ṛgveda Saṃhitā are completed and the work comes to its end. As we indicated in the preface to the first part, this great work is contained in thirty parts comprising 25 thousand pages. It is no exaggeration to say that many learned scholars and researchers, Western ones even more than we Indians, understand well how great is the importance of this Ṛgveda, which is the foundation of all cultures, of the various sciences such as music and literature, and of the Vedānta doctrines. The Ṛgveda, regarded as the most ancient book in the world, and the other Vedas, the Vedic literature and the Upaniṣads have won the recognition and respect of all the civilised countries of the world. But in the research, publication and propagation of such rare and useful works we have not advanced beyond the Westerners. The main reason for this is that these profound works are in the Sanskrit language and are hard for ordinary people—even for Sanskrit scholars—to understand. Corresponding to this, it need hardly be said that the chief cause is the absence of books that present these works in the vernaculars, in a simple style, critically, in accordance with modern science and literature, together with a translation of the meaning.

The first to print this Ṛgveda in England, in the Devanagari script, after carefully examining the old manuscript copies, so that it might be useful to all, was the eminent Western scholar of Germany, Prof. Maxmuller. This work is in the Devanagari script and contains the Saṃhitā and Pada texts with accents, and the Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya separately for each Ṛk. On the basis of this copy, containing the pure text, other Western and Indian scholars have carried out many researches. Since then the Ṛgveda with Sāyaṇa's commentary has also been printed in Bombay and in Calcutta. But they were printed long ago and the copies have run out, so they are not now to be had. Recently, that is in 1933, the Vaidika Saṃśodhana Maṇḍala (Vedic Research Board) of Poona began to print the Ṛgveda with Sāyaṇa's commentary and finished it in four parts in 1946. This is the work generally available now. The price of these four parts is about 100 rupees.

In the works mentioned above the Ṛgveda Saṃhitā, the Pada text and Sāyaṇa's commentary are printed with accents. They contain neither criticism nor explanation of meaning. A Western scholar named Wilson, following Sāyaṇa's commentary, translated only the original text into English.

### Printed p. ii

Apart from a few notes here and there, it contains no extensive explanation or criticism. Even so, this work is useful to students and others. Many years ago in Poona some people printed the Ṛgveda under the name Vedārthayatna, with the Saṃhitā, the Pada text, and explanation in the Marathi language. But this work was not completed; it was printed only as far as the third aṣṭaka and then stopped. Although this work has explanation and criticism of each Ṛk to a small extent, it must be said that they are not enough. In Calcutta, under the leadership of one Satīśa Chandra Seal, M.A., B.L., the Ṛgveda was printed in instalments, useful to all people, with the Saṃhitā, Pada text and Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya, with extensive explanation and criticism in Sanskrit and in English, Hindi and Bengali translations, and various other useful matters. But the printing of this work too stopped at 50 instalments (not even one chapter being complete). One may guess that lack of funds is the reason why the printing of works stops in this way. Apart from these works, it must be said that no attempt has been made to print the Veda in the vernaculars in translation and critically.

Besides this, in English, in the series Sacred Books of the East under the leadership of Prof. Maxmuller, many works relating to Vedic literature and their translations have been printed by Western scholars. In the American Harvard University Series and in the Oxford University Press Series there are many Vedic works and literary works published by Western scholars. In German and French too many Vedic works with criticism and explanation have been published.

It is known to everyone that the translation and publication of such Vedic works, or profound works, is not possible without the generous encouragement and help of governments or universities. For the Ṛgveda with commentary first published (First Edition) by Prof. Maxmuller, the generous financial help of the East India Company was obtained. For the printing of the second edition (Second Edition) the generous patronage of the Maharaja of Vizianagaram was obtained. In Western countries such as America and Germany also, government help for printing such works is always there. In this country too the help and encouragement given by kings and maharajas of many principalities to the publication of Sanskrit works is not small. The maharajas of states such as Baroda, Indore, Kashmir, Kāśī and Travancore, and their governments, have helped greatly in the advancement of Sanskrit works by giving room for the publication of many works in series of their own. In the Mysore country too, through the institution called the Oriental Library (now the Oriental Research Institute), they have published the Kṛṣṇa-yajurveda called the Taittirīya Saṃhitā with the commentary of Bhaṭṭabhāskaramiśra, and other Sūtra works. But that work is now proceeding slowly.

### Printed p. iii

But for the last ten years, under the leadership of our ruler Śrī Jayachamarajendra Wodeyar Bahadur, G.C.B., G.C.S.I., and through his generous encouragement and help, many excellent works that until now existed only in Sanskrit and had not been published in Karnataka and other vernaculars have been translated into the Karnataka language and published under the name Śrī Jayachamarajendra Granthamālā; this is a matter most worthy of praise. In this series the 18 Purāṇas, the 18 Upapurāṇas, works such as the Vāsiṣṭha-Rāmāyaṇa, the Adbhuta-Rāmāyaṇa, the Śaṅkara-Saṃhitā and the Hālāsya-Māhātmya, the Ṛgveda Saṃhitā with translation of the meaning, many Vedic works such as the Mahāpūjākalpa, Gāyatrījapavidhi, Śivapūjāvidhi, Dakṣiṇāmūrtijapavidhāna and Rudramahanyāsa, many hymns composed by Śaṅkarācārya, and hundreds of other works have been printed with their meaning. The translation and printing of a countless number of works, which could not be accomplished even in about 50 years, has been finished under the leadership of the great Prince within a period of just 10 years. The Prince's exceeding interest and learning in Vedic works, in literature and in Vedāntic works, and his weighty good purpose that the benefit of these good works may reach others as well, have helped in accomplishing this. Although all these tasks were carried out with the help of many court scholars and other learned men, the Prince's unrivalled learning and priceless counsel, and his interest and disposition in reading through, from beginning to end, and criticising all the works printed under his patronage, and giving counsel on what matters should be added where and how, and in what manner they should be written, have been the chief cause of the works being published quickly. In the same way, the expectation that under the Prince's generous patronage the remaining three Vedas and other priceless works will soon be published cannot prove vain. On this occasion the aid given by our esteemed Government is also a point worthy of praise.

It will not be out of place to mention briefly here how we have written the arrangement of the translation of the present work, the Ṛgveda Saṃhitā. In the first part, by way of introduction, many matters relating to the Vedas were set out: the Vedas, their importance, their use, the divisions of the Vedas, the Vedic commentaries, the commentators, the Brāhmaṇa work, Āraṇyaka and Upaniṣad belonging to each Veda, the Pada text, the Anukramaṇikā, the seers, the metres, the Nirukta, the various deities, matters concerning those deities, the benefit arising from knowledge of the meaning of the Veda, and many other matters.

Thereafter, for each sūkta, the seer, deity and metre, the viniyoga and other matters connected with it are stated, and before each Ṛk the Saṃhitā text and Pada text are written with accents. Then the Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya for each Ṛk is added there itself. Although the Ṛgveda has many commentaries, such as the Skandasvāmi-bhāṣya, the Veṅkaṭamādhava-bhāṣya, the Udgītha-bhāṣya and the Mudgala-bhāṣya, they are now

### Printed p. iv

not available in full. Only a few portions are found here and there. Moreover, although the Skandasvāmi-bhāṣya, like Sāyaṇa's commentary, is accompanied for each Ṛk by explanation of the meaning, and by necessary stories, history, grammar and other matters as the context requires, the work is not available in full; therefore the useful matters in the one or two aṣṭakas that are available have been collected and added under the heading "Special Topics" (Viśeṣaviṣaya). Although the Veṅkaṭamādhava-bhāṣya has been printed in full, it has neither the detailed explanation nor the meaning following the syntactic connection that Sāyaṇa's commentary has. There is only the meaning of some difficult words and brief notes. Even so, since this commentary and the Skandasvāmi-bhāṣya are older than Sāyaṇa's, the views and opinions of these commentators cannot be disregarded. Therefore we have added them here and there. The Udgītha, Mudgala and other commentaries are likewise available only in a few portions here and there, so their full benefit cannot be obtained. Since only Sāyaṇa's commentary is available in full (for all four Vedas), and since it contains for each Ṛk the translation of the meaning, explanation, Nirukta, earlier history and other useful matters, we have followed Sāyaṇa's commentary. Others have done likewise. It is no exaggeration to say that, apart from Sāyaṇa's commentary, there is in the present day no other means of understanding the Vedas properly. Prof. Maxmuller collected this Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya, which existed in palm-leaf and handwritten copies, with great effort, examined it well with the help of scholars, and printed it in the Devanagari script. Of this work the second edition (Second edition) is freer from faults and more attractive than the first printing. Following this, we have incorporated Sāyaṇa's commentary in this work.

Then for each Ṛk we have added the Pratipadārtha (word-by-word meaning), the Bhāvārtha (gist of the meaning) and the English translation. All these follow Sāyaṇa's commentary. Then in the division called "Special Topics" the special matters relating to that Ṛk—that is, matters concerning the deities, the Nirukta, the derivation of the form of words and their various meanings, stories, earlier history, matters concerning the seers, the opinions of Eastern and Western scholars, relevant matters stated in other Vedas and works (with authority), criticism of the subject, and so on—are explained so that all may understand easily. Lastly, the grammatical derivation is written for each word of each Ṛk. Because words that have occurred once recur again and again, the grammatical derivation has been written only up to the end of the first Maṇḍala (12 parts). Since the occasion arose that the work had to be finished in thirty parts, it has been left off there. Besides these matters, many matters considered indispensable for explaining the meaning of the Ṛgveda have been collected and added here and there. A detailed account of the various deities found in the Ṛgveda and the other Vedas has been added at the end of the 5th part. Likewise the very important work called the Bṛhaddevatā, spoken by the sage Śaunaka, has been added with translation of the meaning and translation at the end of the 28th part. Various portions of the Nirukta, the ancient work spoken by the sage Yāska, which is the first attempt at explaining the meaning of the Ṛgveda and one of the six Aṅgas, have been added with explanation here and there on the relevant occasions. This work is older than three thousand years and is a most important authority (Authority). Without its help the meaning of the Veda cannot be understood. Besides this, the explanations and opinions of Western scholars on various subjects have been added on the relevant occasions.

A Ṛgveda Saṃhitā accompanied by such extensive criticism of subjects has not so far been published in any language of India. One may say confidently that the existence of such a work in the Karnataka language is of great help to the advancement of literature. Although the Ṛgveda Saṃhitā ends here, the work of printing six or seven further parts remains, containing the Aitareya Brāhmaṇa, the Aitareya Āraṇyaka and Upaniṣad, the Nirukta, an alphabetical index (Index) of various subjects, and so on, which belong with it. This work will be undertaken after the sanction of the Government is obtained.

It is our duty to indicate here the part played by the scholars who took part in the making of this work. The president of the committee called the Vedavimarśana Vidvanmaṇḍali, constituted for publishing this work, His Holiness Śrī Śrī Siddhaliṅga Svāmi of the Nāgaliṅga Maṭha, Mysore, took great care in this work and gave his priceless counsel and help. It may be said that this work began through his effort. But as this great man attained union with Śiva about two years ago, he could not see the fruit of his effort completed. Likewise we must mention with deep sorrow that another member of the committee, a scholar of the Mīmāṃsā system named Devara Bhaṭṭa, has also passed away.

Śrī Devaḍu Narasiṃhaśāstri, M.A., of Viśveśvarapura, Bangalore, has written a criticism of some Mīmāṃsā sūtras in this Ṛgvedabhāṣya-bhūmikā. Śrī G. N. Chakravarti, M.A., Lecturer in Sanskrit at St. Philomena's College, Mysore, has written most of the part called Pratipadārtha in this work, and some of the matter in the part called "Special Topics" supported by authorities such as the Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa. Śrī Venkataramanachar, M.A., B.T., a scholar of literature and proficient in the Hindi language, has translated many matters relating to the Veda that are in English and Hindi. The court scholar Br. Śrī Lakṣmaṇācārya, a grammarian, and Br. Śrī G. Viṣṇumūrtibhaṭṭa, a grammarian of His Highness the Maharaja's Sanskrit Mahāpāṭhaśāla, have written the grammar part. Br. Śrī S. Sītārāmaśāstri, retired pandit of the Government Oriental Research Institute, has written some of the part called "Special Topics"

### Printed p. vi

in the part (called "Special Topics"). Br. Śrī Ranganathan, a scholar of literature at the Palace Sarasvatī Bhaṇḍāra, helped greatly in proof-reading and other work while this work was being printed. Besides these, many other scholars have given counsel and help of various kinds. Apart from their help, the Huzur Secretaries and Assistant Secretaries of His Highness the Maharaja, and the staff who assist them, have given the necessary help and co-operation in the publication of this work. It was through the co-operation of these gentlemen that so large a work of 30 parts could be printed so quickly. It is our duty to remember all of them with the deepest gratitude for the help they have given.

Besides this, among those who have supplied many of the books needed for this work and given help and co-operation, the chief institutions are the Sarasvatī Bhaṇḍāra of the Mysore Palace, the Government Oriental Research Institute, the Mysore University and College Library, the Palace Library, and the Madras and Bombay University Libraries. So that this work might be printed quickly, it was entrusted to three leading presses of Mysore to work at the same time. Śrī R. Krishna Shetty, proprietor of the Śrī Śāradā Press on Hanumantha Rao Street, Mysore; Śrī K. Venkatasubbaiah, proprietor of the press called Vinayaka Printing Works in Ramachandra Agrahara, Mysore; and Śrī Y. R. Ranga Rao, proprietor of the Shakti Electric Press (formerly Śrī Chamundeshwari Electric Press) on Hundred-Feet Road, Mysore, took the greatest interest in printing the 30 parts of this work and, with the help of many skilled workmen, day and night alike, finished the printing quickly, handsomely and properly. The Mysore Paper Mills of Bhadravati supplied the paper required for these promptly as needed from time to time. Our gratitude is due to all of them.

Since the translation and publication of this great work demand much money, such a task cannot be carried out without the help and co-operation of kings, maharajas and governments. Just as in the past the East India Company and the Maharaja of Vizianagaram gave generous financial help to Prof. Maxmuller and enabled him to publish the Ṛgveda in the Sanskrit language, so for the welfare of all the people who speak the Karnataka language and for the advancement of literature, in this great undertaking Śrī Jayachamarajendra Wodeyar Bahadur, G.C.B., G.C.S.I., lord of the throne of Karnataka, lover of his people, himself a scholar, devoted to Vedic learning and intent on devotion to God, together with his Government, took the greatest interest, and by his invaluable counsel and encouragement, help and co-operation, caused this work to be brought to completion without obstacle.

### Printed p. vii

In the same way there is no doubt that the generous patronage of the Prince and his Government will be forthcoming in the translation and publication of the other Vedas such as the Yajurveda and of the 108 Upaniṣads. We pray with single-minded devotion to the Lord of all, the Vedapuruṣa, that he may bestow on our ruler Śrī Jayachamarajendra Wodeyar Bahadur, G.C.B., G.C.S.I., so generous of mind and ever intent on works for the good of his people, and on his family, on the princes bearing the auspicious name Śrī Śrīkaṇṭhadatta Narasiṃharāja Wodeyar, on the princesses and the queen mothers, and on the whole body of the people, all auspiciousness such as long life and health, prosperity and wealth, and plenty in the land.

Thus, obedient to the good,

Mysore
Manmatha year, Nija Bhādrapada,
Śukla Caturdaśī, Friday
Dated 30—9—1955

Court Mahāvidvān H. P. Venkata Rao,
Editor & Translator
and
Member of the Vedavimarśana Vidvanmaṇḍali.

### Printed p. 1
Śrī 9. Salutation to Śrī Mahāgaṇādhipati.

> नागीशाद्याः सुमनसः सर्वार्थानामुपक्रमे ।
> यं नत्वा कृतकृत्या स्युस्तं समामि गजाननं ॥
> यस्य निश्वसितं वेदा यो वेदेभ्योऽखिलं जगत् ।
> निर्ममे तमहं वंदे विद्यातीर्थमहेश्वरं ॥
> वेदः शिवः शिवो वेदो वेदाध्यायी सदाशिवः ।
> तस्मात्सर्वप्रयत्नेन वेदमेव सदा जपेत् ॥
> अथाष्टमाष्टकेऽध्यायश्चतुर्थः संप्रदर्शितः ।
> धीमता सायणार्येण पंचमोऽथ प्रदर्शते ॥

Sāyaṇa's Bhāṣya together with the Ṛgveda Saṃhitā, Part 30.

The Fifth Adhyāya of the Eighth Aṣṭaka

The fifth adhyāya in the eighth aṣṭaka: the sūktas of the tenth maṇḍala.

## Sūkta 95 (Maṇḍala 10)

Introduction.

Urvaśī and Purūravas.

This episode, in the form of a dialogue between Urvaśī and Purūravas, is one of the narratives that set forth a supramundane truth by presenting worldly love and separation. Citing an itihāsa, Sāyaṇa has described the occasion of this story in the following way.

### Printed p. 2

> ऐळोर्वशीतिहासोऽत्र वैशद्याय प्रवर्ण्यते ।
> इत्युवर्श्यैळसंवादमिममेषोऽप्यसूचयदिति ॥
> इत्युर्वश्यैळसंवादमिममेषोऽप्यसूचयदिति ॥

(Sāyaṇabhāṣyapīṭhikā. Ṛ. Saṃ. 10-95)

Mitra and Varuṇa were infatuated by Urvaśī's beauty, and through the emission of their seed they became the cause of Agastya's birth; yet they did not fail to curse her. They cursed her, the divine maiden, saying that she should be born on the earth and be united with a mortal.

At the time when a king named Iḷa, together with the sons of Manu, was roaming in a forest for hunting, he entered the place where Umā, the daughter of the mountain-king, who worshipped Śiva with undivided devotion, was dwelling. As the result of that offence he was at once changed into a woman. Then, when he prayed to Śiva, Śiva told him that only Pārvatī had the authority to forgive that offence; thereupon Iḷa took refuge with Pārvatī. As the fruit of his repentance he obtained Pārvatī's forgiveness, and she granted that in every year he should remain a woman for six months, and only during the other six months might he have his own proper form. While he had the form of a woman, Budha, the son of Soma, was infatuated with her and consorted with her. As a result a son was born. He became famous as Purūravas, king of the city Pratiṣṭhāna.

Urvaśī, cursed by Mitra and Varuṇa, descended to the earth as a result of that curse, and while she was roaming she saw Purūravas. Purūravas too, astonished at her beauty, fell in love with her. When he desired union with her, she consented on a condition. "Only when Purūravas is in bed may Urvaśī see him naked and bear it. If she sees him in a state of nakedness at any other time, she will at once go back to her own abode: this is the first rule. Secondly, Purūravas must keep in his charge and protect the two lambs that belong to her": this is the second rule. Purūravas agreed to the condition.

The two lived together and enjoyed a happy life for four years. After four years, one night the bleating of the lambs was suddenly heard. Purūravas rose and looked, and saw that the gods were carrying them off. As he ran in pursuit of them, a flash of lightning appeared at once. Then the naked body of the naked Purūravas became visible to Urvaśī. Instantly she left him and went her way. Purūravas

### Printed p. 3

felt that he had been caught in a fault, and set out searching for her. Wandering with a distraught mind, he saw her sporting with her companions. When he begged her to return and join him, she used harsh words and rejected his prayer. Sāyaṇa states that this conversation between the two is the present narrative occasion. The same episode is told in the Bṛhaddevatā in this way:

> पुरूरवसि राजर्षावप्सरास्तूर्वशी पुरा ।
> न्यवसत्संविदं कृत्वा तस्मिन् धर्मं चचार च ॥

(Bṛ. De. 7-147)

Formerly Urvaśī cultivated love for Purūravas, made an agreement (condition) with him, and lived happily with him. (What that agreement was is not stated. The account here differs from what was said above.)

> तया तस्य च संवासमसूयन् पाकशासनः ।
> पैतामहं चानुरागमिंद्रवच्चापि तस्य तु ॥

(Bṛ. De. 7-148)

Indra could not bear that Purūravas, a mortal, should enjoy happiness with Urvaśī, a divine maiden. Seeing Purūravas occupying Indra's position and enjoying the rank that Indra ought to enjoy, he became jealous and wished to put an obstacle in its way.

> स तयोस्तु वियोगार्थं पार्श्वस्थं वज्रमब्रवीत् ।
> प्रीतिं भिंधि तयोर्वज्र मम चेदिच्छसि प्रियं ॥

(Bṛ. De. 7-149)

Then Indra addressed the thunderbolt that was near him: "O thunderbolt, if you have regard for me and wish to be of benefit to me, use your power and bring about a breach in the love of Urvaśī and Purūravas."**NOTE**

> तथेत्युत्वा तयोः प्रीतिं वज्रोऽभिनस्त्वमायया ।
> ततस्तया विहीनस्तु चचारोन्मत्तवन्नपः ॥

(Bṛ. De. 7-150)

The thunderbolt gave Indra its word that it would obey his command, and by its extraordinary power brought about a breach in the love of Urvaśī and Purūravas. Purūravas, estranged from Urvaśī, wandered about like a madman.

### Printed p. 4

> चरन् सरसि सोऽपश्यदभिरूपामिवोर्वशीं ।
> सखीभिरभिरूपाभिः पंचभिः पार्श्वतो वृतां ॥

(Bṛ. De. 7-151)

As he wandered thus, he saw Urvaśī sporting in a lake. She was sporting together with five female friends whose bodily endowment matched her divine beauty, and so Urvaśī's form shone even more brightly and she was captivating to the mind.

> तामाह पुनरेहीति दुःखात्सा त्वब्रवीन्नृपं ।
> अप्राप्याहं त्वयाद्येह स्वर्गे प्राप्स्यसि मां पुनः ॥

(Bṛ. De. 7-152)

As soon as he saw her, Purūravas addressed her and asked her to come back to him and give him the happiness of union. Unable to accept his call, she spoke thus, in great sorrow: "O Purūravas, you cannot get me back now. When we two meet again in heaven, let us enjoy happiness."

> आह्वानं प्रति चाख्यानमितरेतरयोरिदं ।
> संवादं मन्यते यास्क इतिहासं तु शौनकः ॥

(Bṛ. De. 7-153)

Yāska calls this narrative, which ends in the conversation of Urvaśī and Purūravas, a dialogue (saṃvāda); Śaunaka calls the same episode an itihāsa (story).

The author of the Nirukta, explaining the derivation of the word Purūravas, says:

> पुरूरवाः बहुधा रोरूयते । तस्यैषा भवति ।

(Ni. 10-46)

Because he cried out making a great noise, he became known by the name "Purūravas," one who makes much noise. Having explained this, he cites Ṛk 10. 95. 7 from the Ṛksaṃhitā and speaks about Purūravas. Since this episode conveys more than merely a story, the purport of the mantra cited by the Nirukta author will be given later. Sāyaṇa has briefly told the story of the Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa and shown the connection between that story and the present episode. Before learning the details of that story, it is necessary first to know in which places the story of Urvaśī and Purūravas is told.

Apart from the Ṛksaṃhitā, this story is told in eight different contexts. Those eight occasions are as follows:

### Printed p. 5

1. Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa 11-5-1-15.
2. Kāṭhaka (Yajurveda) 8-10-3.
3. In the commentary of Ṣaḍguruśiṣya on the Sarvānukramaṇī.
4. The story part of the Harivaṃśa, which follows the order of the Vāyu Purāṇa.
5. Viṣṇu Purāṇa 4-6-19.
6. Bṛhaddevatā (this account is given above).
7. Mahābhārata, Ādiparvan (69.21-27).
8. Kathāsaritsāgara 17-4.

Among these, the accounts in the Mahābhārata, the Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa and the Bṛhaddevatā chiefly relate to the present story. The Mahābhārata story is somewhat different. Its details are as follows:

> पुरूरवस्ततो विद्वानिलायां समपद्यत ।
> सा वै तस्याभवन्माता पिता चैवेति नः श्रुतं ॥
> त्रयोदश समुद्रस्य द्वीपानश्नन्नुरूरनाः ।
> अमानुषैर्वृतः सत्वैर्मानुषः सन्महायशाः ॥
> विप्रैः स विग्रहं चक्रे वीर्योन्मत्तः पुरूरवाः ।
> जहार च स विप्राणां रत्नान्युत्तोशतामपि ॥
> सनत्कुमारस्तं राजन्ब्रह्मलोकादुपेत्य ह ।
> अनुदर्शं ततश्चक्रे प्रत्यगृह्णान्नचाप्यसौ ॥
> ततो महर्षिभिः क्रुद्धैः सद्यः शप्पो व्यनश्यत ।
> लोभान्वितो बलमदान्नष्टसंज्ञो नराधिपः ।
> स हि गंधर्वलोकस्थानुर्वश्या सहितो-विराट् ।
> आनिनाय क्रियार्थेऽग्नीन्यथावद्विहितांस्त्रिदा ॥
> षट्‌सुता जज्जिरे चैलादायुर्धीमानमावसुः ।
> दृढायुक्त वनायुश्च शतायुश्चोर्वशीसुताः ॥

(Mahābhārata, Ādiparvan, Adhyāya 69. 21-27)

Purūravas was born in the womb of Iḷā. Iḷā herself stood to him in the form of father and mother; as far as we know, it is said that both parents of Purūravas were in the form of Iḷā.**NOTE** Purūravas, born of her who had such an extraordinary form, ruled as lord of thirteen islands in the midst of the sea. Since he always gained victory

### Printed p. 6

divine beings, pleased with his valour, surrounded this mortal and served him. Grown proud through the power of his valour, he lived in splendour. Out of that very pride he disregarded holy men and persecuted them. Though they resisted many times, he crushed them and seized all their possessions. To put a stop to this ravage, Sanatkumāra once came from the divine world and gave him counsel. But Purūravas did not change his course. Since he went on persecuting holy men in this way, they became angry and cursed him severely. All his power was destroyed, and he became bereft of consciousness. Even so, he brought fire from the world of the Gandharvas for sacrifice and worshipped it. Together with this fire, which is threefold, he brought Urvaśī from the world of the Gandharvas. By her he had six children: 1 Āyu, 2 Dhīrmā, 3 Amāvasu, 4 Dṛḍhāyu, 5 Vanāyu and 6 Śrutāyu — these are the six sons.

Thus the names Āyu, Dṛḍhāyu, Vanāyu and Śrutāyu show that all those of the group called "आयु (*āyu*)" who were born after this progeny are descendants born of Urvaśī and Purūravas. The Vāyu Purāṇa too, like this, describes the wickedness of Purūravas and tells that as its result his destruction came about. The account of the Brahmāṇḍa Purāṇa also follows this. The account is as follows.

"Purūravas's greed for wealth was exceedingly great. However much wealth he gained, he was never satisfied. Once, as he was roaming for hunting, he entered the Naimiṣa forest. In an hermitage in that forest sages were practising austerity. For their sacrifice Viśvakarman had built a sacrificial altar of gold. Purūravas saw that golden altar, and the desire arose in him to seize it. Then the enraged sages struck him with their bundles of darbha grass. That was as severe as the stroke of a thunderbolt, and he collapsed and died." This episode too shows the wickedness of Purūravas. But this wickedness is nowhere described in the Vedic parts. Of all these passages, the account of the Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa shows a direct connection with the story part of the Ṛksaṃhitā. Sāyaṇa has cited the original of the Brāhmaṇa in the introduction (pīṭhikā) to his Bhāṣya. Its purport is given here.

Urvaśī, an Apsaras woman, fell in love with Purūravas, the son of Iḍā. Then she chose him and said, "O Purūravas, you may embrace me three times a day. But you must not lie with me without my consent. Only before me you must appear naked"

### Printed p. 7

…must not (appear). To appear that way is not proper for the dignity of women." With these words she laid down the condition.
(Śa. Brā. 11-5-1-1)
In that same manner she lived with him for a long time, and while living with him she also bore a child. Because she stayed with him so long, the Gandharvas became anxious about her. They thought: "Urvaśī has remained among humans for a very long time. We must find some means of making her come back to us from there." They then brought a ewe with two lambs and tied it to the foot of her bed. After that they carried off one of the lambs.
(Śa. Brā. 11-5-1-2.)
Then Urvaśī, grieved, cried out: "Someone is carrying off my beloved lamb. Is there no hero to protect my lamb? Is this a place without a master?" Meanwhile they seized the second lamb and ran off. Again she cried out in the same piteous way and begged for help.
(Śa. Brā. 11-5-1-3.)
As soon as he heard this cry of distress, Purūravas rose and said: "While I am in this land, how can it be a place without a master? Am I not a hero?" In his haste he leapt out just as he was, naked. Thinking that putting on his clothes would take time, he did not put any on. The Gandharvas, who had been waiting for exactly that moment, produced a flash of lightning. The light, which shone like daylight, appeared, and at once Urvaśī saw Purūravas naked. She instantly vanished. Before Purūravas could say, "Here I have come too," she had gone far away, out of sight. Purūravas, tormented with grief, wandered all around Kurukṣetra. As he wandered, he roamed on the bank of the lake called Anyataḥplakṣa. In that same lake, full of lotus flowers, Urvaśī and her companions were sporting in the form of swans.
(Śa. Brā. 11-5-1-4)
Then Urvaśī recognised him and told her companions, "This is Purūravas." After they had decided, "Let us show ourselves to him," Urvaśī appeared to Purūravas together with her companions.
(Śa. Brā. 11-5-1-5)

### Printed p. 8

This is the introduction to the section of the Ṛk-saṃhitā. The dialogue of the Ṛk-saṃhitā begins at this very point. The same Ṛk-saṃhitā sentences are also recited in the Śatapatha-brāhmaṇa, which carries their conversation forward. But the Brāhmaṇa sentences give only certain parts; the dialogue stated in mantras 1, 2, 14, 15 and 16 of this sūkta of the Ṛk-saṃhitā. Therefore the gist of the dialogue in the Ṛk-saṃhitā is given here briefly.

Because she had suddenly left him and gone away, Purūravas addresses Urvaśī as "O fierce one (O cruel-hearted one)" and wishes to enjoy the pleasure of conversation with her for a little while. In answer she too, displaying her own hardness, says: "O Purūravas, what do you need from this conversation? Like the Uṣas that arose first, I have moved far away from you. I am not to be had by you. Return home." Again Purūravas describes his mental anguish: without Urvaśī's company all his valour has wasted away, he has no strength even to shoot an arrow at his enemies, and he cannot even frighten them. Thus showing his weakness, he pleads again for her company.

She rebukes him: "O Purūravas, you lived freely with me and enjoyed every pleasure. You were born to protect the earth, and you have placed your virility in me. Knowing the future, I told you at the time what your duty was. But you did not heed my well-meant words. Having transgressed my word, why do you now cry out?"

The Ṛk-saṃhitā text has it that after some further exchange of replies she assures him that he will obtain her company in heaven and then disappears. But the Śatapatha-brāhmaṇa states still more:
सा होवाच, संवत्सरतमीग्ं रात्रिमागच्छतात्तन्म एकाग्ं रात्रिमंते शयितासे जात उ तेऽयं तर्हि पुत्रो भवितेति स ह संवत्सरतमीग्ं रात्रिमाजगामेद्धिरण्यविमितानि ततो हैनमेकमूचुरेतत्प्रपद्यस्वेति तद्दास्मै तामुपप्रजिघ्युः॥ (*sā hovāca, saṃvatsaratamīgṃ rātrimāgacchatāttanma ekāgṃ rātrimaṃte śayitāse jāta u te 'yaṃ tarhi putro bhaviteti sa ha saṃvatsaratamīgṃ rātrimājagāmeddhiraṇyavimitāni tato hainamekamūcuretatprapadyasveti taddāsmai tāmupaprajighyuḥ‖*)
(Śa. Brā. 11-5-1-11)
Seeing Purūravas' wretchedness, Urvaśī's heart melted and she speaks thus: "O Purūravas, on the last day of the year come to me and join me; you may be with me for one night only.

### Printed p. 9

A son will be born to you from me." Accordingly he went to her place on the appointed day. While he was seated there on a golden seat, Urvaśī's companions came, sent him up, and sent Urvaśī to him.
सा होवाच । गंधर्वा वै ते प्रातर्वरं दातारस्तं वृणासा इति तं वै मे त्वमेव वृणीष्टेति युष्माकमेवैकोऽसानीति (*sā hovāca | gaṃdharvā vai te prātarvaraṃ dātārastaṃ vṛṇāsā iti taṃ vai me tvameva vṛṇīṣṭeti yuṣmākamevaiko 'sānīti*)
(Śa. Brā. 11-5-1-12)
Urvaśī came to Purūravas and said: "The Gandharvas will grant you a boon in the morning. Accept it, and ask for a boon of your own choice." When Purūravas asked, "Tell me yourself what the boon should be," she said: "Ask them: 'Grant that I too may be a Gandharva together with you.'" Accordingly, the next morning the Gandharvas came and granted Purūravas a boon, and he begged: "Give me the boon that I too may be a Gandharva with you."
ते होचुः ! न वै सा मनुष्येष्टग्नेरज्ञिया तनूरस्ति ययेष्टास्माकमेकः स्यादिति तस्मै ह स्थाल्यामोप्याग्निं प्रददुरनेनेष्टास्माकमेको भविष्यसीति तं च ह कुमारं चादायावप्राज सोऽरण्य एवाग्निं निधाय कुमारेणैव ग्राममेयाय पुनरैमीत्येत्तिरोभूतं योऽग्निरश्वत्थं तं या स्थाली शमिां ताग्ं स ह पुनर्गंधर्वानेयाय (*te hocuḥ ! na vai sā manuṣyeṣṭagnerajñiyā tanūrasti yayeṣṭāsmākamekaḥ syāditi tasmai ha sthālyāmopyāgniṃ pradaduraneneṣṭāsmākameko bhaviṣyasīti taṃ ca ha kumāraṃ cādāyāvaprāja so 'raṇya evāgniṃ nidhāya kumāreṇaiva grāmameyāya punaraimītyettirobhūtaṃ yo 'gniraśvatthaṃ taṃ yā sthālī śamiāṃ tāgṃ sa ha punargaṃdharvāneyāya*)
(Śa. Brā. 11-5-1-13)
"O Purūravas, the principle of sacrifice by which one worships the gods and attains the highest divinity is not yet known to humans. Therefore, unless you obtain that knowledge, you cannot join our company. So we shall teach you this principle of sacrifice." Thus they taught it to him. Having obtained that knowledge, Purūravas attained divinity and joined them. This is the story of the Brāhmaṇa.

In this sūkta no deity is praised. In the sūktas of the Ṛgveda, for the most part, it is prayer praising some deity that is chiefly found. Yet here and there there are other descriptions too: the creation of the world, exposition of principles, praises of kings' gifts, and exposition of other matters. Along with these there are some, such as dialogues relating to the love-play of women and men. Among these the chief are the dialogue of Yama and Yamī, the dialogue of Agastya and Lopāmudrā, the account of Svanaya, and the mutual dialogue of Purūravas and Urvaśī. For love-play

### Printed p. 10

dialogues such as these to have found their place among sūktas that contain only praise of deities is indeed a surprising matter. These mantras may have a hidden meaning other than the one that appears on the surface, or they may have been included here in order to teach a particular moral principle. In very ancient times, that is, when the sūktas of the Ṛgveda had not been divided as they now are, such sūktas and many other kinds of sūkta (with and without praise of deities) may have been current. When the sūktas of the Ṛgveda were arranged in a fixed order, its compilers may have left out many such sūktas, not including them in the Ṛgveda (on the ground that they were not required). Many grounds can therefore be given for the view that not all the sūktas in use at that time are included in the present Ṛgveda; since we shall examine this matter in another context, no explanation on it is needed here. We shall explain briefly, in the form of a dialogue and just as it stands, what is said in the present sūkta.

1. Purūravas—O hard-hearted wife, stay here a little while, showing a mind that indicates mutual love between us. For let us express the feelings in our minds through conversation with each other. If we do not speak with open minds and our longings remain as they are, they will not bring us happiness to the very end.
2. Urvaśī—O Purūravas, what use is there in our talking with each other? Just as the dawn that has passed after sunrise cannot be got back, I have moved far away from you; return to your home. Just as it is impossible to hold the wind in the hand, so I am not to be had by you.
3. Purūravas—O Urvaśī, (I am wasted and wilted by the pain of separation from you; my strength has shrunk.) When I fight for victory, I cannot hurl an arrow with my pressing force. I, who used to rush swiftly upon enemies in battle, am unable to win and bring back the enemies' cattle. I am unable to win the enemies' wealth and the like; in the other royal tasks that I carry out, too, my strength and capacity do not shine, that is, do not stand out. My warriors, who can make enemies tremble with fear in battles, pay no heed to the sound of my command.
4. Urvaśī—(Speaking her own thoughts to the goddess Uṣas) O goddess Uṣas, formerly I lived in the house of my father-in-law, the father of Purūravas, serving him and providing clothing, food and the like. When I longed for my husband,

### Printed p. 11

I would leave my father-in-law's dining hall and go to my husband's quarters. There my husband, embracing me lovingly day and night, would strike me with his male organ so as to please me.
**NOTE**
1. (Addressing Purūravas) O Purūravas, formerly (when I was in your house) you used to delight me three times a day with embraces and the like and with the striking of the male organ. And there I had no trouble from co-wives, that is, rival wives. In this way I lived happily in your house. O hero, at that time you were the lord of my body and of my pleasures and so on, were you not?
6. Purūravas—O Urvaśī, six Apsaras women, named Sujūrṇi, Śreṇi, Summāpi, Pradecakṣu, Granthinī and Caraṇyu, adorned with all ornaments and shining with the golden lustre of their bodies, have come with you. They do not run away as before, and they do not cry out as newly calved cows cry out for shelter.
**NOTE**
7. Urvaśī—As soon as this Purūravas was born, the Apsaras women, or divine women, surrounded and nourished him. Rivers flowing of themselves strengthened him with food and the like. O Purūravas, the gods raised, fed and protected you so that you might fight enemies and, in that battle, destroy the Dasyu people.
8. Purūravas—(Addressing Urvaśī and the other Apsaras women) When this human Purūravas (I) showed his human form before these Apsaras women of divine form, that is, approached them, all those women ran far away from me, like horses yoked to a chariot running swiftly off.
9. When Purūravas, a human, wishes to build contact with these immortal (free from mortality) Apsaras women through suitable words and other acts, those women do not let their forms (bodies) be seen, and they run away like horses roaming and grazing on grass, which, not under the charioteer's control, run off wherever they please.
10. (Addressing Urvaśī alone) This Urvaśī, shining like lightning, descends from the atmosphere and brings the waters that all need. A son, devoted to rites, beneficial to humans and of excellent birth, is born from her. Urvaśī increases my life so that I may live long.

### Printed p. 12

1. Urvaśī—O Purūravas, you were born in the form of a son in order to protect the earth. That is, you have placed your virility, in the form of a son, in my womb. I told you beforehand what is to be done in the future. Yet you did not listen to my word. Since you did not follow my well-meant words, why are you speaking with me?
**NOTE**
12. Purūravas—O Urvaśī, when will the son born in your womb come to me, calling me father? And when will he weep, recognising me as father, to come near? Besides, which son will make the like-minded couple, that is, husband and wife, mutually loving (father and mother), that is, me and you, part? When will the child in your womb, of the nature of radiance (born as a son), delight (your) parents-in-law?
13. Urvaśī—O Purūravas, I shall tell you the answer to the question you asked. As soon as the right time comes, your son will come to you shedding tears and calling you father. I shall send you the child placed in me. Return to your home. O fool, you cannot get me back.
14. Purūravas—(Lamenting in sorrow) Let your husband (I, the husband), who has until now played with you, fall down right here (overcome by a swoon). Or let him go off to a distant land from which he never returns; or let him lie in the lap of Nirṛti, the goddess of evil (of death), that is, let him die; or let wolves rushing swiftly for food devour him.
15. Urvaśī—O Purūravas, (why do you lament in this way?) Do not fall down, do not die; let the terrible wolves not devour you. With women there is no friendship (affection, love) at all; the hearts of those women are, like the hearts of jackals, wholly full of self-interest.
16. O Purūravas, leaving my own true divine form, I lived with you, a human, in human form, roaming at night for four years and dwelling with you. In that time I took a little ghee as food only once. By that alone I was satisfied. Now I have left you and come away.
**NOTE**
17. Purūravas—Urvaśī, who fills and illumines the whole atmosphere with her radiance and is the maker of water, I, the best among the people living on earth, to my side

### Printed p. 13
#### Ṛk 17 (continued)

##### भावार्थ (Bhāvārtha)

…having brought her near me, I will make her subject to my will. May this Purūravas, the doer of good deeds, remain near you (Urvaśī) and enjoy happiness. O Urvaśī, my heart (mind) burns for you. Return quickly.

#### Ṛk 18
Urvaśī: O Purūravas, since you, the son of Ilā, are a human being, you must come under the sway of death. This is the rule made by the gods (the gods themselves say so). Your son (you, born in the form of a son) will satisfy the gods with oblations and the like. As the fruit of this you will join us in heaven and enjoy happiness. This conversation itself is the substance of this sūkta.

Following the story of the Mahābhārata, which was composed on the background of this sūkta, Kālidāsa, famed as a great poet, composed a play called Vikramorvaśīya. But since that belongs to later literature, a critique of it is out of place here.

The opinions of Western scholars on this sūkta are of many kinds. The scholar Wilson writes as follows:

##### English translation
According to the legend narrated by Sayana, Mitra and Varuna, excited by the charms of Urvashi, gave birth to Agastya, pronoucing at the same time an imprecation upon the nymph and condemning her to descend to earth and cohabit with a mortal. Ila, a Raja, having gone out hunting with the sons of Manu came to a place where Uma, the daughter of the mountain, with her attendant nymph, was worshipping Shiva. In punishment for his intrusion he was changed into a woman. Having prayed to Shiva to be restored to manhood, he was referred to Devi who allowed him to be alternately male and female, six months at a time. In the latter condition Budha, the son fo Soma, became cnamoured of her, and had a son by her, who was called Pururavas and was Raja of pratishthana. Pururavas became cnamoured of Urvashi, who became his mistress on condition that if he should be ever naked in her sight except in bed she should return to heaven; he was also to take charge of two pet rams belonging to her. They lived together four years, when one night, hearing the bleating of the rams as they were being carried off by the gods, Pururavas sprang from the bed in pursuit, when a flash of lightning exhibited him to Urvashi naked. She consequently left

### Printed p. 14

##### English translation
him. Pururvas went in quest of her, and wandered about like one distracted. until he at length discovered her sporting with her fellow-nymphs. He impolored her to return. but she refused, calling him by various oppobrious names, as horse, brute, and the like (Sayana merely says Vajra.) This is the occasion of the dialogue of the Sukta, in which, however, Urvashi does not express herself in such unlady–like language, nor are the other particulars of the legend adverted to, beyond an allusion to the past cohabitation of the Raja and the nymph, and the unwillingness of the former to let his mistress depart. Sayana’s story is that of the Puranas. which is evidently different from that of the Veda. The story also of the drama, the Vikramorvashi differs from the Pauranik (being itseif derived from the Mahabharata, Vol I, p. 113; see Professor Cowell’s Translation of the Vikramorvashi , Introd. p. iv, note). Sayana quotes tne Vajasaneyaka. or White Yajush, for the story which varies apparently in some particulars, they not essenially. No Sukta, or portions of a Sukta, corresponding with the present, are found in the Vajasaneyi Samhita.

##### विशेषविषयगळु (Special Topics)

Here is given the etymology that Yāska has explained of the names Purūravāḥ and Urvaśī, who play the chief parts in this sūkta.

Purūravāḥ: पुरूरवाः । पुरूरवा बहुधा रोरूयते । तस्यैषा भवति । (*purūravāḥ | purūravā bahudhā rorūyate | tasyaiṣā bhavati |*) समस्मिञ्चायमान..................देवा (*samasmiñcāyamāna..................devā*) (Si. 10-46, 47) **NOTE**

Durgācārya, the commentator on the Nirukta, explains that this Purūravāḥ is a deity of the middle region (lightning), and that the name arises because it makes much noise (puru—much, rava—sound). To make this meaning clear he cites the 7th Ṛk, समस्मिञ्चायमान (*samasmiñcāyamāna*), and gives the etymology for it. That is explained in the Special Topics of that Ṛk.

Urvaśī: Here is cited the etymology that Yāska gives in discussing the derivation of the words Urvaśī and Apsarāḥ:

उर्वशी । उर्वश्यप्सरा उर्वभ्यश्नुत उरुभ्यामश्नुत उरुर्वा वशोऽस्याः । अप्सरा अप्सारिण्यपि नाप्स इति रूपनामाप्सातेरप्सानीयं भवत्यादर्शनीयं व्यापनीयं वा स्पष्टं दर्शनायेति शाकपूणिः । यदप्प इत्यभक्ष्य । अप्पो नामेति व्यापिनः । तद्रा भवति रूपवती तदनयात्तमिति वा तदस्यै दत्तमिति वा। तस्या दर्शनान्मित्रावरुणयो रेतश्चस्कंद तदभिवादिन्येषर्ग्भवति ॥ (*urvaśī | urvaśyapsarā urvabhyaśnuta urubhyāmaśnuta ururvā vaśo 'syāḥ | apsarā apsāriṇyapi nāpsa iti rūpanāmāpsāterapsānīyaṃ bhavatyādarśanīyaṃ vyāpanīyaṃ vā spaṣṭaṃ darśanāyeti śākapūṇiḥ | yadappa ityabhakṣya | appo nāmeti vyāpinaḥ | tadrā bhavati rūpavatī tadanayāttamiti vā tadasyai dattamiti vā| tasyā darśanānmitrāvaruṇayo retaścaskaṃda tadabhivādinyeṣargbhavati ‖*) (Ni. 5-13)

### Printed p. 15
Urvaśī is a certain apsaras woman. The reason this name comes to this woman is:

(1) उर्वभ्यश्नुते उरु महत् यशोऽभिव्याप्नोतीति (*urvabhyaśnute uru mahat yaśo 'bhivyāpnotīti*). Uru means much fame or renown; she attains (abhyuśnute) it. This woman is widely famous for her great fame (here fame means beauty, charm and the like); this is why she gets the name Urvaśī.

(2) ऊरुभ्यां मैथुने धर्मे पुरुषं अश्नुते व्याप्तीति उर्वाशिनी सती उर्वशीत्युज्यते । (*ūrubhyāṃ maithune dharme puruṣaṃ aśnute vyāptīti urvāśinī satī urvaśītyujyate |*). Ūrū means thighs. By her attractive thighs, in the act of union, she pervades the man with embraces and the like and delights him. Therefore this woman gets the name Urvāśinī or Urvaśī.

(3) अथवा उरुः महान् अस्याः वशः कामः सेयमुरुवशिनी सती उर्वशीत्युच्यते । (*athavā uruḥ mahān asyāḥ vaśaḥ kāmaḥ seyamuruvaśinī satī urvaśītyucyate |*). Uru means much, great. Vaśaḥ means desire of love. Since this woman has very great amorous desire, she gets the name Uruvaśinī or Urvaśī. Thus three kinds of etymology are given for the word Urvaśī.

Now, to explain the meaning of the word Apsarāḥ, its etymology is given. Although this word is not read in this Ṛk, it had to be mentioned in passing while explaining the word Urvaśī; therefore its etymology is given here.

(1) अप्सराः । सा हि अप्सारिणी भवति । अपः पतति नित्यमेव सरति । तत्पभवत्वात् तदेव तस्याः प्रियमुदकं तस्मादप्पराः (*apsarāḥ | sā hi apsāriṇī bhavati | apaḥ patati nityameva sarati | tatpabhavatvāt tadeva tasyāḥ priyamudakaṃ tasmādapparāḥ*). Apsarāḥ means apsāriṇī. Apaḥ means water. She who moves over the water. Since water is very dear to this woman, the name Apsarāḥ has come.

(2) अथवा अद्भ्यः सृतेत्यप्सराः । अपि चैवमन्यथा स्यात् । अप्स इति एतत् रूपनाम । अप्सातेः प्रतिषेधपूर्वस्य प्सातेः भक्षणार्थस्य । तद्धि अप्सानीयं आदनीयं भवति । न हि तद्भक्ष्यते । किं तर्हि । आदर्शनीयं तदाभिमुख्यन स्थित्वा द्रष्टव्यमेव भवति चक्षुषा न पुनर्मुखेन भक्षयि तव्यमित्यर्थः (*athavā adbhyaḥ sṛtetyapsarāḥ | api caivamanyathā syāt | apsa iti etat rūpanāma | apsāteḥ pratiṣedhapūrvasya psāteḥ bhakṣaṇārthasya | taddhi apsānīyaṃ ādanīyaṃ bhavati | na hi tadbhakṣyate | kiṃ tarhi | ādarśanīyaṃ tadābhimukhyana sthitvā draṣṭavyameva bhavati cakṣuṣā na punarmukhena bhakṣayi tavyamityarthaḥ*). Apsaḥ is a name for form. The root psā means "to eat". *A-psā* means not fit for eating **NOTE**. Therefore

### Printed p. 16
the form that is in this woman is not fit to be eaten, but is worthy of being well looked at with the eyes. That is, the intended sense is that this woman's form is very attractive.

(3) व्यापनीयं वा । व्यापनमर्हति । अप्लृ व्याप्तौ तस्य वा अप्सः, तद्धि नायनेन रश्मिना आत्मनः प्रत्ययाधानार्थं व्यापयितव्यं भवति । स्पष्टं दर्शनायेति शाकपूणिः ॥ (*vyāpanīyaṃ vā | vyāpanamarhati | aplṛ vyāptau tasya vā apsaḥ, taddhi nāyanena raśminā ātmanaḥ pratyayādhānārthaṃ vyāpayitavyaṃ bhavati | spaṣṭaṃ darśanāyeti śākapūṇiḥ ‖*). From the root aplṛ vyāptau the word apsaḥ is formed and gives the meaning "pervading". That is, the intended sense is that the form pervades so as to be clearly seen by all and is visible directly.

All these differences of meaning given above accord with the opinion of the teacher Śākapūṇi. Now another meaning of the word Apsarā will be given. It has been said before that apsaḥ means form. Now, according to the root rā dāne, the word rā means "to give"; or, since this form (beauty) has been given to her by Brahmā, the name Apsarā has come; this too is the meaning.

To make the meaning of these words clear, Yāska has given the etymology of the 10th Ṛk of this sūkta, विद्युन्नया पतंती (*vidyunnayā pataṃtī*). That is explained in the Special Topics of that Ṛk.

##### ॥ सायणभाष्यम् ॥ (Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya)

> तत्र हये जाय इत्यष्टादशर्चं पंचमं सूक्तं त्रिष्टुभं । आत्रानुक्रम
> णिका । हये द्योनोर्वशीमैळः पुरूरवाः पूर्वोषितात्कामात्पुनरासाद्यावरोद्भुमैच्छता तमनिच्छंती प्रत्याचष्टे हय इषुर्या कदा सुदेवोऽंतरिक्षप्रां
> सचेति शिस्रष्टैळवाक्यं शिष्वा उर्वश्चा इति। हये जाय इषुर्न श्रिये या सुजूर्णिः
> कदा सूनुः सुदेवो अद्यांतरिक्षप्रां सचा यदासु जहतीष्टित्याद्यास्ति स्रश्चेति नवर्च
> ऐळस्य पुरूरवसो वाक्यानि । आतस्तासां स ऋषिः । शिष्वा नवोर्वश्या वाक्यानि ।
> आतस्तासु सर्षिका । उभयोर्वाक्येषु योऽर्थः प्रतिपाद्यते सा देवता ॥ गतो
> विनियोगः ॥
> ऐळोर्वशीतिहासोऽत्र वैशद्याय प्रवर्ण्यते ।
> मित्रश्च वरुणश्चोभौ दीक्षित् प्रेक्ष्य चोर्वशीं ॥ १ ॥
> रेतः सिषिचतुः सद्यस्तत्कुंभे न्यदधुस्तदा ।
> तां शप्तवंतौ मनुजभोग्या भूम्यां भवेति तौ ॥ २ ॥
> अत्रांतर इळो राजा मनोः पुत्रैश्च संयुतः ।
> मृगयां संचरन् साश्वो देवीक्रीडं विवेश ह ॥ ३ ॥

##### Gist of the bhāṣya

This fifth sūkta, beginning हये जाये (*haye jāye*), has eighteen Ṛks and is in the Triṣṭubh metre. Sāyaṇa quotes the Anukramaṇikā: Aila Purūravas, desiring Urvaśī, who had formerly lived with him, approached her again and wished to take her back; she, not wishing it, rejects him. Of the verses, nine are the words of Aila Purūravas, and Purūravas is their seer; the nine that remain are the words of Urvaśī, and she is their seer. The subject set forth in the words of each is that verse's deity. The viniyoga has been given already.

Then, to make it clear, he tells the history of Aila and Urvaśī in verse. Mitra and Varuṇa, both consecrated, saw Urvaśī and at once let fall their semen and placed it in a pot; and they cursed her: "Be on earth, to be enjoyed by a mortal." Meanwhile King Ilā, with Manu's sons, roaming on a hunt on horseback, entered the sporting-ground of the Goddess

> अत्र वाजसनेयकं । उर्वशी हास्पराः पुरूरवसॆमैळं चकमे तग्ं ह विंदमानोवाच त्रिः स्म माहो नैतसेन दंडेन हतादकामा स्म मा निपद्यास्यॆ मो स्म त्वा
> नग्नं दर्शमेष वै न स्त्रीणामुपचार इति सा हास्मिइज्योगुवासापि हास्माद्गर्भिण्यास
> तावज्यो ग्घास्मिन्नुवास । ततो ह गंधर्वाः समूदिरे ज्योग्या इयमुर्वशी
> 2

##### Gist of the bhāṣya

…where the mountain's daughter (Pārvatī) pleased the god with every mood. Ilā entered, and since it had been said there, "Whoever enters here shall become a woman," he became a woman, and, distressed, went at once to Śiva for refuge. Śambhu told the king, "She must be propitiated." He went to the Goddess for refuge to gain his manhood; the Goddess made him, after six months, one who had gained manhood. Then at a certain time Budha, bewitched by beauty, joyfully united with Ilā, who surpassed the apsarases. From Ilā, the king Purūravas was born, known as son of the son of Soma. Urvaśī loved him as he lived in the city of Pratiṣṭhāna. She laid down a condition: apart from the bed she was not to see him naked, otherwise she would go back as she came; and he was to keep two rams as her sons near her. Having made this agreement she delighted the king. When four years had passed, one night the gods carried off the two rams; hearing their sound, the king rose naked and went after them, saying "Come, I have conquered them"; but lightning showed him naked to her, and Urvaśī, her condition seen, went to heaven. Then the king, like a madman, searched about hoping to see her, and on the shore of Lake Mānasa saw her sporting with the apsarases. He again desired to enjoy her. She, tearful, saw him and, spoken to, rejected him: "Go." Thus he too indicated the dialogue of Aila and Urvaśī.

Here the Vājasaneyaka (Brāhmaṇa) is cited: उर्वशी हास्पराः पुरूरवसॆमैळं चकमे तग्ं ह विंदमानोवाच त्रिः स्म माहो नैतसेन दंडेन हतादकामा स्म मा निपद्यास्यॆ मो स्म त्वा नग्नं दर्शमेष वै न स्त्रीणामुपचार इति सा हास्मिइज्योगुवासापि हास्माद्गर्भिण्यास तावज्यो ग्घास्मिन्नुवास । ततो ह गंधर्वाः समूदिरे ज्योग्या इयमुर्वशी (*urvaśī hāsparāḥ purūravasèmaiḷaṃ cakame tagṃ ha viṃdamānovāca triḥ sma māho naitasena daṃḍena hatādakāmā sma mā nipadyāsyè mo sma tvā nagnaṃ darśameṣa vai na strīṇāmupacāra iti sā hāsmiijyoguvāsāpi hāsmādgarbhiṇyāsa tāvajyo gghāsminnuvāsa | tato ha gaṃdharvāḥ samūdire jyogyā iyamurvaśī*). Urvaśī, an apsaras, loved Purūravas Aila; finding him she said: "Three times a day you shall strike me with a rod, but not against my wish; do not lie with me [otherwise], and let me not see you naked; this is the way of women." She lived with him for a long time, even pregnant by him. Then the Gandharvas said among themselves: "This Urvaśī has lived long among men."
